I mean, I definitely get very worked up and uncomfortable when I’m worrying, but it’s more like it builds and then just kind of stays with me, if that makes sense. I’ll get caught in it and keep thinking through all the possibilities.\n\nBut those sudden, out-of-nowhere episodes where I feel like intense fear hits all at once—no, I can’t really say I’ve noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding whole places or activities exactly, but I am putting things off because of the worry. At work, for example, even pretty straightforward listing negotiations or contract reviews are taking me much longer than they used to. I’ll read something, then go back and read it again, and then I’ll think, “Well, what if I missed a clause, or what if I didn’t explain this clearly enough?” So something that should be routine ends up eating up way more of my time.\n\nAnd I’ve delayed returning calls too, which is embarrassing to admit because in real estate you’re supposed to be responsive. But sometimes I don’t feel confident that I’ve checked everything carefully enough before I call someone back, so I wait until I’ve gone over it again. It’s not that I’m intentionally hiding from clients, but the effect is kind of the same—I hesitate, and things get slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like headaches, muscle tension, or difficulty sleeping associated with your worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, the muscle tension for sure. I notice it most by the end of the day—my neck and shoulders feel really tight, almost like I’ve been bracing myself for something all day without realizing it. It’s that kind of stiffness where I’ll finally sit down and think, “Why do I feel like I’ve been holding my shoulders up to my ears?”\n\nAnd I catch myself clenching my jaw too. Sometimes I’m driving between listings or sitting at my desk going over paperwork, and suddenly I realize my teeth are pressed together. I don’t always notice it while I’m doing it, but then once I do, it’s like, oh, I’ve probably been sitting like this for twenty minutes. It seems to happen more when my mind is running through all the things I’m worried I might have missed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel on edge or restless when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I do feel unsettled when I’m worrying, but I don’t know if I’d call it “restless” exactly. It’s more like my mind won’t let the issue drop, and I have this sense that I should be doing something about it—checking something, thinking it through again, making sure I haven’t overlooked anything.\n\nSo yes, maybe “on edge” fits somewhat, but it’s not like I’m pacing around the room all the time or unable to sit still. It’s more internal, if that makes sense. I can look calm on the outside, but inside I feel like I’m waiting for some problem to show up because I didn’t catch it soon enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any changes in your mood, such as feeling sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of feeling sad or depressed, at least not that I’ve really noticed. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, and sometimes I feel worn down by how much mental energy everything takes, but I wouldn’t describe it as a low mood exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I’m preoccupied or tense, not sitting around feeling hopeless or crying or anything like that. I still care about my work and my family and normal things. I just sometimes feel annoyed that my mind won’t quiet down. So… no, I don’t think depression is really the word I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Is there any area of your life where you feel you don't worry excessively?\n\nPatient: Honestly, that’s part of why I came in. I’m having a hard time finding an area where I don’t worry lately. It feels like it spreads into almost everything.\n\nWith work, I worry about clients, whether I’ve handled everything correctly, whether something will fall through, whether the market is going to shift in a way I should have anticipated. Then outside of work, I worry about my family, and money, and even things that should probably be settled already—like whether moving to the condo was really the right decision. I’ll go back over it in my head even though, practically speaking, there may not be any new information.\n\nSo I guess there are moments when I’m distracted or busy and not actively worrying, but in terms of whole areas of my life that feel free of it… not really. It feels out of proportion sometimes, like the level of worry doesn’t match what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasurable due to your worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve stopped enjoying things, exactly. I still can enjoy normal things when I’m actually doing them, like spending time with my family or getting absorbed in something pleasant. It’s just that the worrying can be sort of… running in the background, if that makes sense.\n\nSo if you mean, have I lost interest in things or they feel completely flat, then no, I don’t think that’s really happening. But sometimes it takes me longer to settle into enjoying something because part of my mind is still going over whatever I was concerned about. It’s not that the activity itself isn’t pleasurable anymore—it’s more that I’m distracted from it.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been interfering with your relationships or daily life in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, especially at work. I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart or anything like that, but I can tell it’s slowing me down.\n\nLike, things that used to be fairly routine for me—straightforward listing negotiations, going through contracts, checking details I’ve reviewed a hundred times before—are taking much longer than they used to. I’ll read something, then go back and read it again, and then start wondering if I missed some small clause or didn’t think through a possible issue carefully enough.\n\nAnd because of that, I’ve delayed returning calls sometimes, not because I’m ignoring people, but because I don’t feel ready yet. I feel like I need to check one more thing before I can confidently call back, and then that “one more thing” turns into several more things. It’s frustrating, because I know how to do my job, and I’ve done it for years, but lately the worrying makes even normal tasks feel heavier and more time-consuming than they should.\n\nDoctor: Is there even one thing in your daily life where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free? I’m honestly having trouble thinking of even one thing. I mean, there are moments where I’m occupied and maybe not actively thinking about it, but if you ask whether there’s an area of my daily life that feels untouched by worry… not really.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It feels like the worry has spread everywhere—clients, whether I handled something correctly, what the market might do, my family, money. Even the condo, which should probably just be something I’ve accepted by now, I still catch myself thinking, “Was that the right move? Did we make the best decision?” And there isn’t always a real reason for it in the moment. It just starts up.\n\nSo no, I can’t say there’s one thing that feels completely free of it. Sometimes I know the amount I’m worrying doesn’t really match what’s actually happening, but I can’t seem to just set it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is distressing. I think that’s really what pushed me to come in, because it feels like I’m worrying about almost everything now, and I can’t always point to something happening that justifies how worked up I feel about it.\n\nI worry about clients, whether I handled a situation correctly, what the market is going to do, whether I’m missing something financially, my family, money in general… and then even decisions that are already made, like moving to the condo, I’ll still find myself going back over it in my head, wondering, “Was that really the right decision?” It’s not as if there’s some new crisis with it every day, but my mind treats it like there is.\n\nAnd I do recognize, at least part of the time, that the amount of worry is out of proportion to what’s actually happening. That almost makes it more frustrating, because I can see that, but I still can’t seem to stop going over it. So yes, it bothers me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying tends to persist even after you've resolved or completed the task that triggered it?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts of it, because even when something is technically done, my mind doesn’t seem to treat it as done.\n\nI’ll finish a task or resolve whatever the immediate issue was, and I’ll tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent. You handled it. Let it go.” But then one concern kind of turns into another. I’ll start replaying a small detail—something I said, or a line in a document, or whether I should have worded something differently—and before I know it, I’ve spent half an hour going over something I really meant to put down.\n\nIt’s not always a huge dramatic worry, either. Sometimes it’s just these little loops, like my brain keeps looking for something I might have missed. And I know, logically, that I already dealt with the task, but emotionally it doesn’t feel settled right away. So yes, the worrying can keep going even after the thing that started it is over.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you feel you worry about less than other things in your life?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe there are small things I worry about less, but I’m having a hard time separating them out, because lately it feels like the worry sort of attaches itself to almost everything.\n\nLike, I might worry less about something routine if it doesn’t involve a client or money or my family, but even then my mind can find a way in. The big ones are definitely clients, the market, financial decisions, my family, and then even things that should be settled already, like the condo. I keep going back to, “Was that really the right decision?” even when nothing new has happened to make me question it.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether there’s an area that feels clearly lighter than the rest, I’d say maybe little everyday errands or very simple tasks are a bit less intense. But overall, I came in because it feels like I’m worrying about everything now, and a lot of the time it feels bigger than what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about the future limited to specific areas of your life or is it more of a general feeling that applies to almost everything you think about?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I’d say. I mean, there are definitely areas that are louder than others—clients, the market, money, my family, those kinds of things—but it doesn’t stay neatly in one box anymore.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in, honestly. It feels like I’m worrying about almost everything now. Even things that should be settled, like moving to the condo, I’ll still circle back and think, “Was that actually the right decision?” And it’s not always because something new has happened. Sometimes nothing has changed at all, but my mind still starts treating it like there’s a problem I need to solve.\n\nSo yes, there are specific topics, but the feeling is broader than that. It just feels out of proportion to what’s actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been affecting your personal relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I think it affects things more at work than with family or friends, at least in the most obvious way. With family, I’m sure I can be preoccupied, but what I notice most is how much it slows me down professionally.\n\nEven straightforward listing negotiations or contract reviews—things I’ve done for years and should be able to move through pretty efficiently—are taking me much longer than they used to. I’ll keep going back over the same sections or replaying the terms in my head, like I’m afraid I’ve missed something obvious. And then I end up delaying calls I should return, not because I don’t want to talk to people, but because I don’t feel confident I’ve checked everything carefully enough yet.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I know how to do my job. I’ve been doing this a long time. But lately I don’t always trust that I’ve reviewed something enough, even when it’s a pretty standard situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly with daily functioning at work. I mean, I still get things done, but it takes so much longer than it used to. Even a pretty straightforward listing negotiation or a contract review can turn into this whole drawn-out process because I keep rereading things and checking them again, thinking, “What if I missed something?”\n\nAnd then that affects calls, too. I’ll put off returning a call because I don’t feel ready yet, like I haven’t checked the file carefully enough or I need to be absolutely sure before I speak to the client. It’s not that I don’t know how to do the work—I do—but lately I don’t feel confident moving through it the way I used to.\n\nWith relationships, I think the work side is where it shows up the most clearly. But yes, in terms of daily tasks, especially professional ones, it’s definitely slowing me down.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying significantly impacted your ability to function in your daily life or maintain your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I guess I’d say it’s most obvious in my work day rather than, you know, every single part of my life.\n\nThe work tasks that used to feel routine don’t feel routine anymore. Like listing negotiations or reviewing contracts—I’ve done those for years, and I know what I’m looking at—but now they can take me much longer than they should because I keep going back over them. I’ll reread the same section, check the same details, and then still have this feeling like, “But what if I missed something?” It’s not that the work is necessarily more complicated. It’s more that I don’t trust myself to move on.\n\nAnd then that starts to affect other things, like returning calls. I’ve delayed calling people back because I feel like I need to check the file one more time before I talk to them. Then one more time becomes several times, and before I know it I’ve lost time and I’m frustrated with myself.\n\nSo yes, it is affecting my functioning. I’m still doing my job, but it takes more effort and more time than it used to, and I don’t feel as efficient or confident as I used to.\n\nDoctor: Is there a single issue that you worry about more than any other, or does your worry seem to spread across many different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It spreads, definitely. I wish I could say it was just one thing, like only work or only finances, but it doesn’t really stay in one lane anymore.\n\nI came in because I feel like I worry about everything now—clients, the market, my family, money. Even the condo, which was supposed to be a sensible move, I’ll find myself going over and over whether that was the right decision. And sometimes there isn’t even anything actually happening in that moment to justify how much I’m worrying. It’s like my mind takes a normal concern and then keeps stretching it out until it feels much bigger than it should.\n\nSo no, it’s not one single issue. It’s more like the worry jumps from one area to another, and I have a hard time putting it down once it starts.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I feel like I worry about everything lately, and I mean everything. Like I’ll take something small and my mind just keeps turning it over and turning it over. I’m here because it feels out of proportion, and I’m tired of feeling like I can’t just trust that things are basically okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it suddenly spikes and peaks within a few minutes. I mean, I definitely get very worked up and uncomfortable when I’m worrying, but it’s more like it builds and then just kind of stays with me, if that makes sense. I’ll get caught in it and keep thinking through all the possibilities.\n\nBut those sudden, out-of-nowhere episodes where I feel like intense fear hits all at once—no, I can’t really say I’ve noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding whole places or activities exactly, but I am putting things off because of the worry. At work, for example, even pretty straightforward listing negotiations or contract reviews are taking me much longer than they used to. I’ll read something, then go back and read it again, and then I’ll think, “Well, what if I missed a clause, or what if I didn’t explain this clearly enough?” So something that should be routine ends up eating up way more of my time.\n\nAnd I’ve delayed returning calls too, which is embarrassing to admit because in real estate you’re supposed to be responsive. Sometimes I’m driving between listings or sitting at my desk going over paperwork, and suddenly I realize my teeth are pressed together. I mean, I do feel unsettled when I’m worrying, but I don’t know if I’d call it “restless” exactly. It’s more like my mind won’t let the issue drop, and I have this sense that I should be doing something about it—checking something, thinking it through again, making sure I haven’t overlooked anything.\n\nSo yes, maybe “on edge” fits somewhat, but it’s not like I’m pacing around the room all the time or unable to sit still. It’s more internal, if that makes sense. I can look calm on the outside, but inside I feel like I’m waiting for some problem to show up because I didn’t catch it soon enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any changes in your mood, such as feeling sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of feeling sad or depressed, at least not that I’ve really noticed. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, and sometimes I feel worn down by how much mental energy everything takes, but I wouldn’t describe it as a low mood exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I’m preoccupied or tense, not sitting around feeling hopeless or crying or anything like that. I still care about my work and my family and normal things. I just sometimes feel annoyed that my mind won’t quiet down. So… no, I don’t think depression is really the word I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Is there any area of your life where you feel you don't worry excessively?\n\nPatient: Honestly, that’s part of why I came in. I’m having a hard time finding an area where I don’t worry lately. It feels like it spreads into almost everything.\n\nWith work, I worry about clients, whether I’ve handled everything correctly, whether something will fall through, whether the market is going to shift in a way I should have anticipated. Then outside of work, I worry about my family, and money, and even things that should probably be settled already—like whether moving to the condo was really the right decision. I’ll go back over it in my head even though, practically speaking, there may not be any new information.\n\nSo I guess there are moments when I’m distracted or busy and not actively worrying, but in terms of whole areas of my life that feel free of it… not really. It feels out of proportion sometimes, like the level of worry doesn’t match what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasurable due to your worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve stopped enjoying things, exactly. I still can enjoy normal things when I’m actually doing them, like spending time with my family or getting absorbed in something pleasant. It’s just that the worrying can be sort of… running in the background, if that makes sense.\n\nSo if you mean, have I lost interest in things or they feel completely flat, then no, I don’t think that’s really happening. But sometimes it takes me longer to settle into enjoying something because part of my mind is still going over whatever I was concerned about. It’s not that the activity itself isn’t pleasurable anymore—it’s more that I’m distracted from it.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been interfering with your relationships or daily life in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, especially at work. I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart or anything like that, but I can tell it’s slowing me down.\n\nLike, things that used to be fairly routine for me—straightforward listing negotiations, going through contracts, checking details I’ve reviewed a hundred times before—are taking much longer than they used to. I’ll read something, then go back and read it again, and then start wondering if I missed some small clause or didn’t think through a possible issue carefully enough.\n\nAnd because of that, I’ve delayed returning calls sometimes, not because I’m ignoring people, but because I don’t feel ready yet. I feel like I need to check one more thing before I can confidently call back, and then that “one more thing” turns into several more things. It’s frustrating, because I know how to do my job, and I’ve done it for years, but lately the worrying makes even normal tasks feel heavier and more time-consuming than they should.\n\nDoctor: Is there even one thing in your daily life where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free? I’m honestly having trouble thinking of even one thing. I mean, there are moments where I’m occupied and maybe not actively thinking about it, but if you ask whether there’s an area of my daily life that feels untouched by worry… not really.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It feels like the worry has spread everywhere—clients, whether I handled something correctly, what the market might do, my family, money. Even the condo, which should probably just be something I’ve accepted by now, I still catch myself thinking, “Was that the right move? Sometimes I know the amount I’m worrying doesn’t really match what’s actually happening, but I can’t seem to just set it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is distressing. That almost makes it more frustrating, because I can see that, but I still can’t seem to stop going over it. So yes, it bothers me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying tends to persist even after you've resolved or completed the task that triggered it?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts of it, because even when something is technically done, my mind doesn’t seem to treat it as done.\n\nI’ll finish a task or resolve whatever the immediate issue was, and I’ll tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent. You handled it. Let it go.” But then one concern kind of turns into another. I’ll start replaying a small detail—something I said, or a line in a document, or whether I should have worded something differently—and before I know it, I’ve spent half an hour going over something I really meant to put down.\n\nIt’s not always a huge dramatic worry, either. Sometimes it’s just these little loops, like my brain keeps looking for something I might have missed. And I know, logically, that I already dealt with the task, but emotionally it doesn’t feel settled right away. With family, I’m sure I can be preoccupied, but what I notice most is how much it slows me down professionally.\n\nEven straightforward listing negotiations or contract reviews—things I’ve done for years and should be able to move through pretty efficiently—are taking me much longer than they used to. I’ll keep going back over the same sections or replaying the terms in my head, like I’m afraid I’ve missed something obvious. And then I end up delaying calls I should return, not because I don’t want to talk to people, but because I don’t feel confident I’ve checked everything carefully enough yet.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I know how to do my job. I’ve been doing this a long time. But lately I don’t always trust that I’ve reviewed something enough, even when it’s a pretty standard situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly with daily functioning at work. I mean, I still get things done, but it takes so much longer than it used to. I’ll put off returning a call because I don’t feel ready yet, like I haven’t checked the file carefully enough or I need to be absolutely sure before I speak to the client. But yes, in terms of daily tasks, especially professional ones, it’s definitely slowing me down.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying significantly impacted your ability to function in your daily life or maintain your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I guess I’d say it’s most obvious in my work day rather than, you know, every single part of my life.\n\nThe work tasks that used to feel routine don’t feel routine anymore. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose it does in a roundabout way, but when you say “free time” or “being present,” I still think the biggest problem starts during the workday.

What happens is, even things that should be very straightforward for me—like listing negotiations or reviewing a contract—take me much longer than they used to. I’ll go through something, and then I’ll go back through it again, and then I’ll think, “Well, did I really check that carefully enough?” So I’ll check it again. And it isn’t that I don’t know how to do the work. I do. I’ve done it for years. It’s more that I don’t feel settled enough to move on.

And then that spills over because I’ll delay returning calls. I’ll think, “I should call them back, but let me just look at the file one more time first,” because I don’t feel confident that I’ve checked everything carefully enough to answer properly. Then suddenly I’ve spent all this extra time on something that should have been handled already.

So by the time I’m done, yes, I’m more worn out and probably less present afterward. But I’d say the main way it’s affecting me is that work is taking longer and I’m second-guessing myself more than I used to.

It’s not that I’m intentionally hiding from clients, but the effect is kind of the same—I hesitate, and things get slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like headaches, muscle tension, or difficulty sleeping associated with your worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, the muscle tension for sure. I notice it most by the end of the day—my neck and shoulders feel really tight, almost like I’ve been bracing myself for something all day without realizing it. It’s that kind of stiffness where I’ll finally sit down and think, “Why do I feel like I’ve been holding my shoulders up to my ears?”\n\nAnd I catch myself clenching my jaw too. Sometimes I’m driving between listings or sitting at my desk going over paperwork, and suddenly I realize my teeth are pressed together. Then outside of work, I worry about my family, and money, and even things that should probably be settled already—like whether moving to the condo was really the right decision. I’ll go back over it in my head even though, practically speaking, there may not be any new information.\n\nSo I guess there are moments when I’m distracted or busy and not actively worrying, but in terms of whole areas of my life that feel free of it… not really. It feels out of proportion sometimes, like the level of worry doesn’t match what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasurable due to your worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve stopped enjoying things, exactly. I still can enjoy normal things when I’m actually doing them, like spending time with my family or getting absorbed in something pleasant. It’s just that the worrying can be sort of… running in the background, if that makes sense.\n\nSo if you mean, have I lost interest in things or they feel completely flat, then no, I don’t think that’s really happening. But sometimes it takes me longer to settle into enjoying something because part of my mind is still going over whatever I was concerned about. It’s not that the activity itself isn’t pleasurable anymore—it’s more that I’m distracted from it.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been interfering with your relationships or daily life in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, especially at work. I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart or anything like that, but I can tell it’s slowing me down.\n\nLike, things that used to be fairly routine for me—straightforward listing negotiations, going through contracts, checking details I’ve reviewed a hundred times before—are taking much longer than they used to. I’ll read something, then go back and read it again, and then start wondering if I missed some small clause or didn’t think through a possible issue carefully enough.\n\nAnd because of that, I’ve delayed returning calls sometimes, not because I’m ignoring people, but because I don’t feel ready yet. I feel like I need to check one more thing before I can confidently call back, and then that “one more thing” turns into several more things. It’s frustrating, because I know how to do my job, and I’ve done it for years, but lately the worrying makes even normal tasks feel heavier and more time-consuming than they should.\n\nDoctor: Is there even one thing in your daily life where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free? I’m honestly having trouble thinking of even one thing. I’ll keep going back over the same sections or replaying the terms in my head, like I’m afraid I’ve missed something obvious. And then I end up delaying calls I should return, not because I don’t want to talk to people, but because I don’t feel confident I’ve checked everything carefully enough yet.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I know how to do my job. I’ve been doing this a long time. But lately I don’t always trust that I’ve reviewed something enough, even when it’s a pretty standard situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly with daily functioning at work. I mean, I still get things done, but it takes so much longer than it used to. Even a pretty straightforward listing negotiation or a contract review can turn into this whole drawn-out process because I keep rereading things and checking them again, thinking, “What if I missed something?”\n\nAnd then that affects calls, too. I’ll put off returning a call because I don’t feel ready yet, like I haven’t checked the file carefully enough or I need to be absolutely sure before I speak to the client. It’s not that I don’t know how to do the work—I do—but lately I don’t feel confident moving through it the way I used to.\n\nWith relationships, I think the work side is where it shows up the most clearly. Like listing negotiations or reviewing contracts—I’ve done those for years, and I know what I’m looking at—but now they can take me much longer than they should because I keep going back over them. I wish I could say it was just one thing, like only work or only finances, but it doesn’t really stay in one lane anymore.\n\nI came in because I feel like I worry about everything now—clients, the market, my family, money. It feels like it spreads into almost everything.\n\nWith work, I worry about clients, whether I’ve handled everything correctly, whether something will fall through, whether the market is going to shift in a way I should have anticipated. Then outside of work, I worry about my family, and money, and even things that should probably be settled already—like whether moving to the condo was really the right decision. I’ll go back over it in my head even though, practically speaking, there may not be any new information.\n\nSo I guess there are moments when I’m distracted or busy and not actively worrying, but in terms of whole areas of my life that feel free of it… not really. It feels out of proportion sometimes, like the level of worry doesn’t match what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasurable due to your worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve stopped enjoying things, exactly. I still can enjoy normal things when I’m actually doing them, like spending time with my family or getting absorbed in something pleasant. It’s just that the worrying can be sort of… running in the background, if that makes sense.\n\nSo if you mean, have I lost interest in things or they feel completely flat, then no, I don’t think that’s really happening. But sometimes it takes me longer to settle into enjoying something because part of my mind is still going over whatever I was concerned about. It’s not that the activity itself isn’t pleasurable anymore—it’s more that I’m distracted from it.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been interfering with your relationships or daily life in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, especially at work. I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart or anything like that, but I can tell it’s slowing me down.\n\nLike, things that used to be fairly routine for me—straightforward listing negotiations, going through contracts, checking details I’ve reviewed a hundred times before—are taking much longer than they used to. I’ll read something, then go back and read it again, and then start wondering if I missed some small clause or didn’t think through a possible issue carefully enough.\n\nAnd because of that, I’ve delayed returning calls sometimes, not because I’m ignoring people, but because I don’t feel ready yet. I feel like I need to check one more thing before I can confidently call back, and then that “one more thing” turns into several more things. It’s frustrating, because I know how to do my job, and I’ve done it for years, but lately the worrying makes even normal tasks feel heavier and more time-consuming than they should.\n\nDoctor: Is there even one thing in your daily life where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free? I’m honestly having trouble thinking of even one thing. I mean, there are moments where I’m occupied and maybe not actively thinking about it, but if you ask whether there’s an area of my daily life that feels untouched by worry… not really.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It feels like the worry has spread everywhere—clients, whether I handled something correctly, what the market might do, my family, money. Even the condo, which should probably just be something I’ve accepted by now, I still catch myself thinking, “Was that the right move? Did we make the best decision?” And there isn’t always a real reason for it in the moment. It just starts up.\n\nSo no, I can’t say there’s one thing that feels completely free of it. Sometimes I know the amount I’m worrying doesn’t really match what’s actually happening, but I can’t seem to just set it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is distressing. I think that’s really what pushed me to come in, because it feels like I’m worrying about almost everything now, and I can’t always point to something happening that justifies how worked up I feel about it.\n\nI worry about clients, whether I handled a situation correctly, what the market is going to do, whether I’m missing something financially, my family, money in general… and then even decisions that are already made, like moving to the condo, I’ll still find myself going back over it in my head, wondering, “Was that really the right decision?” It’s not as if there’s some new crisis with it every day, but my mind treats it like there is.\n\nAnd I do recognize, at least part of the time, that the amount of worry is out of proportion to what’s actually happening. That almost makes it more frustrating, because I can see that, but I still can’t seem to stop going over it. So yes, it bothers me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying tends to persist even after you've resolved or completed the task that triggered it?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts of it, because even when something is technically done, my mind doesn’t seem to treat it as done.\n\nI’ll finish a task or resolve whatever the immediate issue was, and I’ll tell myself, “Parin, this is not urgent. You handled it. Let it go.” But then one concern kind of turns into another. I’ll start replaying a small detail—something I said, or a line in a document, or whether I should have worded something differently—and before I know it, I’ve spent half an hour going over something I really meant to put down.\n\nIt’s not always a huge dramatic worry, either. Sometimes it’s just these little loops, like my brain keeps looking for something I might have missed. And I know, logically, that I already dealt with the task, but emotionally it doesn’t feel settled right away. So yes, the worrying can keep going even after the thing that started it is over.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you feel you worry about less than other things in your life?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe there are small things I worry about less, but I’m having a hard time separating them out, because lately it feels like the worry sort of attaches itself to almost everything.\n\nLike, I might worry less about something routine if it doesn’t involve a client or money or my family, but even then my mind can find a way in. The big ones are definitely clients, the market, financial decisions, my family, and then even things that should be settled already, like the condo. I keep going back to, “Was that really the right decision?” even when nothing new has happened to make me question it.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether there’s an area that feels clearly lighter than the rest, I’d say maybe little everyday errands or very simple tasks are a bit less intense. But overall, I came in because it feels like I’m worrying about everything now, and a lot of the time it feels bigger than what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about the future limited to specific areas of your life or is it more of a general feeling that applies to almost everything you think about?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I’d say. I mean, there are definitely areas that are louder than others—clients, the market, money, my family, those kinds of things—but it doesn’t stay neatly in one box anymore.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in, honestly. It just feels out of proportion to what’s actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been affecting your personal relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I think it affects things more at work than with family or friends, at least in the most obvious way. And then I end up delaying calls I should return, not because I don’t want to talk to people, but because I don’t feel confident I’ve checked everything carefully enough yet.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I know how to do my job. But lately I don’t always trust that I’ve reviewed something enough, even when it’s a pretty standard situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly with daily functioning at work. I mean, I still get things done, but it takes so much longer than it used to. Even a pretty straightforward listing negotiation or a contract review can turn into this whole drawn-out process because I keep rereading things and checking them again, thinking, “What if I missed something?”\n\nAnd then that affects calls, too. I’ll put off returning a call because I don’t feel ready yet, like I haven’t checked the file carefully enough or I need to be absolutely sure before I speak to the client. It’s not that I don’t know how to do the work—I do—but lately I don’t feel confident moving through it the way I used to.\n\nWith relationships, I think the work side is where it shows up the most clearly. But yes, in terms of daily tasks, especially professional ones, it’s definitely slowing me down.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying significantly impacted your ability to function in your daily life or maintain your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I guess I’d say it’s most obvious in my work day rather than, you know, every single part of my life.\n\nThe work tasks that used to feel routine don’t feel routine anymore. Like listing negotiations or reviewing contracts—I’ve done those for years, and I know what I’m looking at—but now they can take me much longer than they should because I keep going back over them.
